# Daily Papers
The project automatically fetches the latest papers from arXiv based on keywords.

The subheadings in the README file represent the search keywords.

Only the most recent articles for each keyword are retained, up to a maximum of 100 papers.

You can click the 'Watch' button to receive daily email notifications.

Last update: 2026-10-20

| **[Coordination Matters: Process-Aware Evaluation of Cooperative Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06557v2)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Cooperative multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) benchmarks commonly emphasize aggregate outcomes such as return, success rate, or completion time. While essential, these metrics can obscure how agents coordinate and fail to distinguish policies that achieve similar outcomes through different interaction processes. We propose a process-aware evaluation framework that complements aggregate performance with diagnostics of interference, coordinated task completion, participation, and coordination efficiency. We evaluate the framework in STAT, a controlled spatial task-allocation testbed, and examine its broader applicability in two established cooperative MARL benchmarks. Across diverse value-based and actor-critic methods, we find policies with nearly identical return can exhibit substantial differences in coordination behavior. Controlled scaling in STAT reveals distinct process signatures for spatial, task, and agent scaling, while a reward-shaping ablation shows that explicitly penalizing assignment conflicts can alter coordination behavior with little change in task return. Results in the established benchmarks further show that the framework remains informative under different coordination mechanisms. These findings support process-aware evaluation as a complement to outcome-based benchmarking. The framework provides a common structure for selecting and interpreting environment-specific diagnostics that characterize not only what cooperative policies achieve, but how they coordinate to achieve it.</p></details> | Under review |
| **[CocoRNA: Collective RNA Design with Cooperative Multi-agent Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23820v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Designing RNA sequences that reliably fold into specific secondary structures is essential for understanding their biological functions but remains a challenging computational problem. We propose CocoRNA, a cooperative multi-agent reinforcement learning framework for RNA inverse design. CocoRNA simplifies the design task by decomposing it into smaller sub-problems, each solved collaboratively by multiple agents. This approach reduces the complexity of the problem and improves the exploration of design policies. During training, a centralized critic uses global structural information to guide the agents, enabling them to jointly optimize their design strategies. As a result, CocoRNA learns high-quality RNA design policies that generalize effectively to unseen structures without additional training. Experiments on the Rfam dataset demonstrate that CocoRNA substantially outperforms state-of-the-art methods in both success rate and design speed. Further experiments on other biological sequence design tasks highlight the effectiveness and broad potential of CocoRNA for complex design tasks.</p></details> | ICML 2026 |
| **[Jarvis: Coordination Deserves a Brain of Its Own in Agentic Interactive Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23804v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Interactive models increasingly hand complex reasoning and tool use to a slow brain in the background, leaving a realtime front model free to sustain full-duplex dialogue. Yet the user may refine, cancel or replace a request while its task runs, so a plan or result that made sense a moment ago goes stale. Each new utterance must therefore be read against the work in flight, but the front model follows only the conversation and the back end sees only its own task. Coordination deserves a brain of its own, one that sees both. We introduce Jarvis, a coordination harness where models judge and code enforces. Its typed decision model, JEV, reads each turn against the live tasks and picks among operations built from a versioned task record. Code checks every choice against the current state before applying it, so no decision can act on a task version the user has replaced. Jarvis also rechecks plans before they execute and results before they are spoken, and counts an answer as delivered only once the user has heard it in full. We build two instances of this design. Jarvis-Audio runs unchanged on three full-duplex speech models. Jarvis-Omni adds vision and rechecks the evidence behind an answer before and during playback, since a scene can change while nobody speaks. With the front model and back end held fixed, replacing Qwen Audio Agent with Jarvis raises tau-Voice task success more than sixfold, from 29 to 192 of 278 tasks, and the gains hold on every benchmark and front model. Against published systems, Jarvis-Audio reaches 69% Pass1 on Full-Duplex-Bench v3 with an open 9B front model, versus 60% for GPT-Realtime. These results suggest that how the two brains are coordinated matters as much as how strong they are.</p></details> |  |
| **[DrugHarness: An Evolvable Agent Harness for Computational Drug Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23791v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Computational drug discovery requires agents to compose scientific operations and adapt to changing evidence and constraints. Here we introduce DrugHarness, an evolvable agent harness coupling composable scientific execution with conditional skill induction. Its environment integrates over 80 scientific operations, biomedical resources and task-specific code, preserving associations among candidates, results and artifacts. From execution trajectories and outcome feedback, DrugHarness induces action hypotheses with observable applicability conditions, recommended actions and supporting evidence. An independent review agent evaluates candidate skills derived from these hypotheses through source verification and, when needed, targeted execution trials. Admitted skills guide subsequent tasks and are revised through feedback. Evaluation on MolBench and SMDD-Bench demonstrates capabilities in molecular screening, optimization and constrained design. Without evolution, DrugHarness exceeds reported MolClaw-CC results by 10.8 percentage points in affinity-comparison accuracy and 55% in mean top-three active hits on MolBench. Retrospective evaluation on skill-development tasks shows that evolution increases Qwen's affinity-comparison accuracy from 62.2% to 81.1% and optimization success from 79.5% to 87.2%. In a prospective HPK1 study, DrugHarness achieves a more favorable median docking score than Biomni among system-specific candidates. A synthesized compound achieves a cellular IC50 of 14.19 nM, a 2.64-fold potency improvement over the best input reference, with activity competitive with two selected compounds designed by experienced medicinal chemists. These results support conditional skill evolution for scientific agents and demonstrate a workflow connecting computational drug design with experimental validation.</p></details> |  |
| **[DLite: History-free Speculative Decoding for Agentic AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23787v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Speculative decoding has become a mainstream technique for accelerating large language model (LLM) inference by using a lightweight draft model to generate candidate tokens that are subsequently verified by the target model. However, agentic workloads introduce long and continuously evolving contexts and frequent multi-turn interactions. Under such workloads, we observe that existing draft models suffer from up to an 80% degradation in acceptance length, while maintaining their historical KV cache introduces substantial system overhead. In this paper, we identify the growing historical context as a common source of both problems and introduce DLite, a history-free speculative decoding framework for agentic AI. DLite directly reuses contextual hidden states from the target model and performs local speculative prediction without explicitly maintaining the historical context, transforming an increasingly complex modeling problem into local simple prediction problem. A lightweight sequential head further restores intra-block causal dependencies with low overhead. To effectively train this heterogeneous history-free architecture, we introduce a tailored two-stage training strategy. Experiments on general-purpose, long-context and agentic workloads show that DLite achieves 2-6 acceptance length and outperforms the state-of-the-art speculative decoding method, DSpark, up to 70%.</p></details> | 14 pages, 5 figures |
| **[Enforcing Code Reasoning in Agentic Scaffold for More Effective Issue Resolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23782v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reasoning is central to ReAct-based programming agents, yet whether and how they reason about code remains unclear. We study 7,955 issue-resolution trajectories from six single- and multi-agent scaffolds across four programming languages on SWE-bench-Verified, SWE-bench-Pro, and SWE-bench-Lite. We identify 12 forms of code reasoning in 66.95% of trajectories. Code reasoning is associated with higher resolution rates, and its intensity increases with task complexity. However, it remains largely unguided: agents may focus on surface-level symptoms during localization, fail to refine near-correct patches, overlook issue requirements, and generate tests with limited behavioral diversity. To address these limitations, we propose DeskCheck, an agentic scaffold that systematically guides code reasoning throughout repository-level issue resolution. DeskCheck simulates execution paths to localize root causes, simulates candidate patches to refine near-misses, audits patches against issue requirements, and reasons about corner-case inputs and expected outputs before generating tests. On 731 SWE-bench-Pro instances, DeskCheck improves resolution rates over mini-SWE-Agent and Claude Code by 6.29% to 10.53%, at an additional cost of $0.02-$0.28 per instance (average: $0.14). Per-phase analyses show higher method-level localization recall, more successful repairs of imprecisely localized issues, and better detection of missed corner cases in near-correct patches. Exact McNemar tests show statistically significant improvements across all subject LLMs (p < 10^-10), while repeated experiments support robustness to agent nondeterminism. These findings motivate systematic code reasoning as a means of improving programming agents.</p></details> |  |
| **[GRAFT-ATHENA: Self-Improving Agentic Teams for Autonomous Discovery and Evolutionary Numerical Algorithms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.11117v3)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Language-model agents plan, execute, and evaluate scientific workflows. However, the problem--method relationships guiding their choices remain implicit, so plausible outputs can violate the assumptions of the scientific problem. We introduce Graph Reduction to Adaptive Factored Trees (GRAFT), which makes this problem-to-method map explicit as an expandable probabilistic structure of admissible problems, methods, and dependencies. Because this structure follows scientific semantics, knowledge from documentation, papers, and domain experts can be encoded, while factorization keeps the structure tractable. Semantic fingerprints of encoded problems measure their similarity, so expertise and experience guide related problems. GRAFT-guided workflows exceeded published expert baselines in physics-informed learning, recovered blood-rheology trends, predicted Apollo Command Module hypersonic loading within 1.8\% of experiment, and established machine-checked error bounds for regularized brain-flow reconstruction and a spectral physics-informed architecture. The representation retains evaluated outcomes and incorporates new methods, allowing scientific knowledge and experience to accumulate.</p></details> |  |
| **[Agentic AI for Heterogeneous System Design Across Chip, Scale-Up, and Scale-Out Domains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23724v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The design space of modern AI/HPC systems has expanded rapidly across compute architectures, including GPUs, dataflow processors, and RISC-V accelerators; memory tech- nologies, including HBM, GDDR, and SRAM; and interconnects such as NVLink, UALink, Infinity Fabric, InfiniBand, Slingshot, and Ultra Ethernet. No single architecture is optimal across LLM training, LLM serving, and AI-coupled HPC workloads, while the resulting combinatorial design space is too large for manual exploration. We present ArchAgent, a hierarchical multi- agent framework in which an LLM-based planner decomposes system design into chip, scale-up, and scale-out subproblems, each handled by specialist agents. A deterministic analytical evaluator closes the loop, ensuring that all performance estimates are derived from analytical models rather than generated by the LLM. On a mixed AI+HPC workload under a 1 MW power budget, ArchAgent discovers heterogeneous designs that achieve a 1.93x weighted geometric mean speedup over a homogeneous GPU baseline, while requiring only 18 evaluations compared with 100 evaluations for hill climbing, which achieves 1.65x speedup.</p></details> |  |
| **[GLOBE: Evolving Structured Agentic Memory for E-commerce Product Optimization in Generative Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23709v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Optimizing product listings for generative search requires language agents not only to rewrite effectively but also to continually self-improve by retaining successful strategies across diverse categories. However, existing approaches often treat structured listings as free-form text, leaving effective strategies implicit in prompts, search trajectories, or rewritten outputs rather than accumulating them as a reusable agentic memory. To address this limitation, we introduce GLOBE, an agentic framework that drives continual self-improvement by storing optimization knowledge in an evolving handbook of explicit, field-scoped behaviors. In each episode, an Editor applies relevant behaviors and receives rank-based feedback, which a Reflector converts into behavior updates. Crucially for dependable execution in real-world settings, a Moderator removes redundant proposals, a Curator commits accepted changes, and consistently harmful behaviors are deactivated. This cycle transforms episodic feedback into a persistent, field-scoped optimization memory. Experiments show that structured behaviors outperform monolithic prompt optimization, while feedback-driven evolution and moderation further improve effectiveness and reduce downside risk. The learned behaviors also remain effective on unseen product categories, suggesting that agentic memory evolution supports interpretable and transferable product optimization.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to the Second Workshop for Research on Agent Language Models(REALM) at EMNLP 2026</p></details> |
| **[Tool Recommendation for Multi-Step Agents: Finding Every Tool a Task Needs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23707v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A multi-step agent task fails when a single tool it needs is missing, yet the tools an agent may need are too many to list: the roughly 8,200 tools on the platform we study have definitions that together exceed the context window of any model we evaluate. We present Tool Recommendation, a deployed system that gives an agent all of them, hundreds of times more than a typical MCP server exposes, through a fixed interface of three tools. It runs at the gateway between agent and tools, executing every call under the end user's own credentials and checking it against the user's consent for each service and the tools the agent may use. We evaluate it on both a public retrieval benchmark and billed end-to-end session cost. On ToolRet, Tool Recommendation places every tool a query needs in the top ten 57% more often than BM25, a lexical ranking that commercial tool search offers, and compares favorably with published systems up to nine times its size. In paired multi-step workflows, its first recommendation covered every required tool in 93% of Codex sessions, and against a fixed list of 100 tools it cut input tokens by 17% with Codex and 40% with Claude Code and the total bill in all 14 runs, by up to 40%, with no change in task completion.</p></details> | <details><summary>11 pa...</summary><p>11 pages, 3 figures, 5 tables</p></details> |
| **[Agentic Physics-Grounded Video Generation via Memory-Augmented In-Context Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23677v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Physical plausibility is essential for realistic video generation. Existing methods impose physical guidance before synthesis via simulations or refined prompts, but this can limit dynamic diversity, miss errors that emerge only after generation, and require repeated regeneration when inconsistencies remain. We therefore study a generate-diagnose-repair paradigm that preserves the original generation capability and selectively repairs physical inconsistencies afterward, raising two key questions: what to repair and how to repair? To address them, we introduce PhysRepair, an agentic video generation framework that combines causal reasoning over the observed physical failure with prior repair experience to enable localized and adaptive correction. Specifically, Spatiotemporal Causal Constraints identify the first invalid physical state and its causally affected states, producing a repair specification that guides physically plausible local regeneration while preserving unaffected content. Experience-augmented In-context Learning combines the ongoing repair context with relevant experience from previous repairs, allowing the agent to adapt its repair decisions across diverse physical failures and accumulate completed trajectories for future cases. Experiments on Physics-IQ Verified across solid mechanics, fluid dynamics, optics, thermodynamics, and magnetism demonstrate that PhysRepair consistently improves the physical plausibility of generated videos over existing methods. Our project page is available at https://xxkkxxx.github.io/PhysRepair-Website/.</p></details> | <details><summary>12 pa...</summary><p>12 pages, 5 figures, 1 table</p></details> |
| **[On the possibilities and limitations of LLM-based multi-agent systems for multi-criteria decision-making in healthcare operations management](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23673v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-criteria models for healthcare operations need weights that express how stakeholders trade off patient waiting time, provider workload, and operating cost, but these weights are difficult to quantify. Teams of large language model (LLM) agents, each prompted to represent a stakeholder, have been used to supply them. We propose a framework for assessing the reliability of LLM-generated preferences as inputs to multi-objective optimization and quantifying how their uncertainty affects decisions. We study explicit preferences by asking agents to rate the importance of the criteria in an outpatient appointment scheduling problem, and implicit preferences by inferring trade-offs from the agents' pairwise choices between emergency department configurations. The computational experiments show that the explicit preferences respond to the clinic's operating conditions and differ across roles, but deliberation among the agents can erase these differences, whereas simple averaging preserves them. The pairwise choices used to reveal the agents' implicit preferences depend largely on which option is presented first, and we find limited evidence that a statistical model using contextual information alongside presentation order predicts the agents' choices on new comparison pairs better than a model using presentation order alone. We then formulate robust weighted-sum optimization models to account for preference uncertainty. In most cases, these models select different schedules or configurations from those obtained using average preference weights. These results indicate that LLM-generated preferences need an audit of this kind before they are used as optimization weights in healthcare decision support.</p></details> |  |
| **[ABF-Bench: A Verifiable Benchmark for Agentic Blockchain Forensics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23656v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Blockchain forensics is adversarial and open-ended: money launderers split, swap, and bridge funds, cut their trail at mixers or custodial services, and change tactics from one incident to the next. Investigators must keep adapting their tracing in response. Yet the immutability of public ledgers preserves every recorded movement in public view, making much of the investigation independently verifiable. We introduce ABF-Bench, an interactive benchmark that makes evidence-backed fund tracing a verifiable agent task, in which every claimed movement must cite ledger records the agent retrieved. A case-to-task pipeline builds well-posed tasks across ledgers: conservative rules halt tracing wherever the records leave a continuation unresolved, and each task's reference graph is the answer those rules determine. Built from real laundering activity, the tasks combine multi-hop transfers, DeFi exchanges, cross-chain round trips, custodial and mixer deposits, and address poisoning. We also propose the ABF agent harness, built around a working memory, and evaluate it alongside general-purpose agents and fixed-order tracers. The harness reaches 75% edge recall, against 47% for Codex with the same model and 10% for Claude Code, which loses recall to overconfidence: it cites records it left unretrieved. Compared with fixed-order tracers, the harness acquires evidence faster than the best of them early in the budget and recognizes undisclosed custodial services that they walk past (89% vs. 0%), with 98% fidelity. However, agents tend to stop early and lose recall on larger tasks, which holds their final recall below the best tracer. Beyond exposing these gaps, ABF-Bench opens blockchain forensics to LLM agents as a verifiable domain, where the verdicts that score agents also offer potential training signals.</p></details> |  |
| **[Where Do Multi-Agent Systems Fail? Evidence-Grounded Diagnosis of Collective Mechanisms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38761v2)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>When a multi-agent system answers correctly, it is tempting to conclude that its agents shared, checked, and used information as intended. Yet a system can break one of its collective mechanisms, the rules that govern how agents route, admit, store, and act on shared information, and still return the right answer, while a wrong answer rarely reveals which mechanism failed. We ask what evidence from an execution is sufficient to conclude that a particular mechanism was violated. Our answer is a diagnostic contract, which separates what counts as a violation from which execution records can establish one, and concludes that a violation is supported, ruled out, or unknown; removing records can make this conclusion unknown but never reverse it. We test contracts for four mechanisms by replaying executions from the step where a mechanism acts, once unchanged, once with the mechanism broken, and once with it restored. Broken mechanisms often left the answer correct. An LLM diagnoser detected many more violations from internal records than from public outputs, yet with identical records a generic prompt often claimed certainty the records did not support, which prompts stating the contracts largely avoided. The contracts also applied, in narrow form, to mechanisms in independently developed systems, but a diagnostic behavior that was nearly perfect on our benchmark degraded on an independently developed workflow. A correct outcome is therefore no substitute for records of how collective mechanisms operated, and agreement on one benchmark does not show that a diagnoser transfers to another system.</p></details> |  |
| **[SIT-Eval: Situation-Based Interactive Turn-by-Turn Evaluation of Conversational Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23612v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The rapid proliferation of conversational AI agents necessitates evaluation methods that assess performance across diverse dialogue scenarios, utilizing multiple situational criteria, as appropriate behavior depends on context and encompasses various aspects. Existing dialogue benchmarks typically use static natural conversations, which underrepresent rare situations---a critical safety gap, as low-probability events become frequent failures at production scale. However, limited sample sizes force a choice: either maintain natural dialogues for realism or turn to adaptive situation sampling for comprehensive yet statistically reliable coverage. We introduce SIT-Eval, an adjustable framework for evaluating situation-based conversational agents. We formalize an agent's desired behavior as situational rules: if situation is P, follow rules Q. At each conversation turn, an LLM-interlocutor enacts a uniformly sampled situation. A context-aware LLM-judge leverages all conditionally applicable rules as criteria to evaluate the agent's reply for that situation. The judge is validated using human-verified synthetic golden dialogues. Benchmarking 8 LLMs on 1292 conversation turns reveals failure modes hidden by aggregate metrics: models with similar overall scores vary widely in their adherence to individual rules. All models struggle most with ambiguity clarification and Socratic questioning.</p></details> |  |
| **[From Trajectories to Differentiated Skills: Cognitive Differentiation for Self-Improving Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23599v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents tackle complex tasks through reasoning and interaction with external environments. Agent skills provide persistent, transferable capabilities that guide task planning, inform contextual decisions, support tool use, and enable executable procedures. Yet existing skill-evolution methods largely organize knowledge around skill artifacts, overlooking the functional heterogeneity within individual skills and its implications for knowledge maintenance and use. Inspired by cognitive science, we propose Cognitive Differentiation in Skill Evolution (CDSE), which evolves a unified skill through four functionally distinct yet coordinated components: Meta, Experience, Tool, and Script. CDSE extracts evidence-grounded semantic gradients from successful and failed trajectories, consolidates them by functional role, and applies component-specific updates and validation with cross-component coordination and adaptive invocation. An analysis of 500 top-ranked skill packages shows that 96.8% contain knowledge spanning at least two functional roles. Across six benchmarks, three agent models, and two execution harnesses, CDSE achieves the highest scores in all 36 settings, improving over the Base agent by 7.6 percentage points and the strongest competing method in each setting by 2.6 points on average. Ablation studies further support the importance of functional differentiation and adaptive invocation.</p></details> | <details><summary>22 pa...</summary><p>22 pages, 7 figures, 17 tables, including appendices</p></details> |
| **[RL-Guided Temporal Localization for Dual-Channel Retrieval in Long-Horizon Agent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23598v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Time-aware retrieval is crucial for long-horizon LLM agents to effectively leverage ever-growing long-term memory. Existing methods focus on lexical or semantic similarity, leading to target memories being crowded out by temporally mismatched yet semantically similar candidates. Even time-aware approaches inject temporal cues only during memory organization or answer generation, overlooking temporal constraints at the retrieval stage, where candidate selection occurs - the root cause of the displacement. Inspired by the temporal contiguity effect in human episodic memory, we propose TIDER (Temporal Interval-driven Dual-channel Evidence Retrieval), a time-aware agentic retrieval framework. Central to TIDER is a reinforcement learning (RL)-trained temporal interval locator that infers the temporal windows in which the evidence lies. RL optimizes window localization as a discrete, non-differentiable retrieval decision, using composite rewards to convert sparse answer supervision into fine-grained retrieval feedback. These temporal windows first define the temporal retrieval channel; the retrieved candidates are then merged with results from the semantic channel and reranked by proximity to the windows. This temporal grounding enables TIDER to retrieve temporally aligned evidence that would otherwise be drowned out by the volume of semantically similar yet temporally mismatched memories. With a lightweight 1.7B locator, TIDER achieves state-of-the-art accuracy on both long-horizon memory benchmarks - 61.52% on LifeBench (+8.82%) and 78.96% on LoCoMo (+3.66%) - which indicates that temporal cues are crucial for pinpointing target evidence amid a vast pool of semantically similar historical events in long-term daily-life memory.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |
| **[Darwin-Claw: Environment-Adaptive Experience Consolidation for Recursively Self-Evolving Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23574v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>While Large Language Model (LLM) agents have demonstrated remarkable capabilities across complex tasks, they predominantly operate in isolated sessions, lacking the ability to continually learn from historical interactions or adapt to user-specific environments. Existing self-evolution paradigms often indiscriminately inject raw interaction trajectories into memory or skill modules, leading to obscured behavioral patterns and compromised update controllability. Furthermore, as foundational LLM reasoning improves, the bottleneck for agentic evolution shifts from accumulating generic knowledge to effectively managing situational experience. To address this, we propose Darwin-Claw, an environment-adaptive self-evolving framework that systematically distills episodic experiences to drive the comprehensive evolution of operational skills and durable memory. Theoretically grounded in an iterative agent-actor and evolver-critic co-adaptation, Darwin-Claw introduces a Hierarchical Experience Consolidation System that extracts fine-grained error attributions and generalizes them into dynamically updated prototype vectors. This structured situational experience serves as interaction patches for context-sensitive perception and explicit optimization signals for skill refinement. Practically, Darwin-Claw is a readily deployable, automated framework compatible with mainstream agent systems without requiring manual curation. Extensive experiments on WildClawBench demonstrate that Darwin-Claw yields substantial performance improvements, establishing a controllable and stable pathway for the continual, user-centric adaptation of autonomous agents in real-world ecosystems.</p></details> |  |
| **[Mizzle: A Complete Concurrent Incorrectness Logic with Applications to Agentic Bug Certification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11611v2)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models are increasingly used to find bugs in real-world programs, but they also produce a flood of false alarms that waste developers' time. We propose to prevent these false alarms by requiring an LLM to accompany each bug report with a machine-checked proof, in a program logic, that the reported bug is real. Unlike a test, such a proof is unaffected by how rarely a concurrency bug manifests, and can certify a bug against a library's specification rather than its implementation. We follow the approach of incorrectness logics, whose under-approximate reasoning establishes that a claimed behavior is genuinely reachable, and hence a true positive. In our case, however, the logic must model a realistic programming language, be mechanized so that proofs can be checked, and be complete, so that no real bug is ruled out for want of a derivation. We present Mizzle, an incorrectness separation logic for concurrent programs written in a substantial subset of OCaml, parametric in the notion of incorrectness. We mechanize Mizzle in the Rocq proof assistant on top of the Iris framework, and we prove that it is both sound, so that it never justifies a false alarm, and complete, so that every incorrect execution admits a derivation. We instantiate Mizzle with three notions of incorrectness: stuckness (triggering undefined behavior), the presence of a memory race (in a sequentially consistent setting), and the non-linearizability of a data structure. We show that an LLM agent can use Mizzle to certify seeded memory races and linearizability bugs for under a dollar per bug on average. We also report two previously unknown linearizability bugs in an OCaml library of concurrent data structures.</p></details> |  |
| **[SkillBrowser: Selective Skill Exposure for Scalable Skill-Using Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23554v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model agents increasingly rely on skills to extend their capabilities in complex tasks. However, scalable skill use requires Selective Skill Exposure (SSE): providing relevant procedural content when needed while avoiding unnecessary exposure that may disrupt reasoning. Existing paradigms fall short in complementary ways. Progressive disclosure exposes metadata for a growing skill library, introducing context pollution; retrieval limits catalog exposure, but static retrieval from the initial task query may be misaligned with capability needs that emerge during reasoning and may still inject unnecessary skills. We propose SkillBrowser, a Meta-Skill-managed framework that enables an agent to identify current capability needs, formulate targeted queries, and selectively load skills on demand. Across six SRA-Bench subsets and five LLMs, SkillBrowser improves aggregate accuracy over static retrieval and LLM-based selection, including multi-step tool use, and mitigates accuracy degradation on already-solvable instances. Benefits remain model- and task-dependent, with under-invocation of useful skills and additional latency as key limitations. These results demonstrate the viability of Meta-Skill-managed SSE for scalable skill repositories.</p></details> | <details><summary>23 pa...</summary><p>23 pages, 3 figures. Submitted to ACL Rolling Review, October 2026</p></details> |
| **[MLOps-Bench: Benchmarking AI Agents on Production ML Engineering in Real Repositories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23506v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI agents are increasingly expected to modify and maintain machine-learning systems in production, yet existing benchmarks primarily evaluate either competition-style ML experimentation or generic repository issue resolution. We introduce MLOps-Bench, a repository-grounded benchmark for production ML engineering. Unlike prior ML-agent benchmarks, MLOps-Bench evaluates executable lifecycle changes inside real ML repositories, spanning data pipelines, feature engineering, model training, model serving, monitoring, CI/CD and governance, and end-to-end integration. Each task is grounded in repository evidence and includes a natural-language requirement, oracle patch, plausible buggy baseline, fail-to-pass tests, pass-to-pass tests, and provenance metadata. The benchmark contains 212 validated tasks from 31 public ML repositories, with 636 fail-to-pass tests and 856 pass-to-pass tests. We evaluate six agents and find that the best system solves 31.2% of tasks while passing 68.7% of tests, showing that current agents often make partial progress but still fail production-relevant behavioral requirements. MLOps-Bench shifts agent evaluation beyond model building and issue fixing toward repository-native MLOps work where correctness depends on lifecycle reasoning, behavioral robustness, and preservation of existing system behavior.</p></details> |  |
| **[Teach the Magnitude, Not the Direction: Verifier-Anchored Credit Assignment for Multi-Turn Multi-Step LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13179v2)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) directly optimizes verifier-defined outcomes for multi-turn tool-use agents, yet its trajectory-level credit assignment conflates heterogeneous per-turn outcomes into a single reward signal. On-policy distillation provides dense per-token supervision but uses a teacher-defined token-level objective and can be prone to gradient concentration collapse. We introduce $\textbf{CrEST}$, a hierarchical credit assignment framework that anchors each sampled token's scalar advantage sign to verified rewards while incorporating dense magnitude signals from a privileged self-teacher. $\textbf{CrEST}$ resolves credit at two levels: turn-segmented verified advantages address inter-turn dilution, while entropy-gated self-teacher modulation refines intra-turn token contributions. On Hunyuan-A13B, it edits 43.24% of retained checkpoints while matching KEEP's observed 91.89% continuation success and reducing unconditional mean continuation prompt cost by 4,619.8 tokens (2.13%). | **[AgentOdyssey: Open-Ended Long-Horizon Text Game Generation for Test-Time Continual Learning Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24893v2)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>For agents to learn continuously from interaction with the world at test time, they must be able to explore effectively, acquire new world knowledge and skills, retain relevant episodic experiences, and plan over long horizons. Critically, AgentOdyssey goes beyond the conventional machine learning assumption that learning does not occur at test time by placing agents in a continuous, long-horizon setting that interleaves learning and inference throughout deployment. We further propose a multifaceted evaluation methodology that measures not only game progress but also offers diagnostic tests on world knowledge acquisition, episodic memory, object and action exploration, action diversity, and model cost. We evaluate diverse agent paradigms across six generated games. Our experimental results reveal critical limits in agents' key abilities, as well as factors that influence their meaningful horizon. Although performance scales with stronger base models, even the top agent remains far below human performance on a demanding long-horizon game, leaving substantial headroom for improvement. Additionally, a focused study of agent test-time training finds that, although short-term memory is beneficial, agents trained with supervised fine-tuning still suffer from catastrophic forgetting, suggesting that effective parametric continual learning remains an open challenge for test-time agents.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to NeurIPS 2026 | Project page: https://agentodyssey.github.io/</p></details> |

Four models contributed approximately an equal share at every positive risk level, and four of the five also contributed when risk was zero, even though contributing could not improve their payoff. Explicitly stating that only the agent's own payoff mattered reduced zero-risk contributions for most models, while a more neutral prompt did not. Several models correctly identified which action offered the higher expected payoff at different risk levels, yet their contributions changed little. Payoff-based selection also depended on population composition: in a single six-agent group, it favoured the model that contributed least and reduced group success, whereas in larger, randomly mixed populations, it favoured an exact fair-share model at moderate and high risk. Our evaluation of 14 representative models reveals substantial limitations: the highest recall and exact-match rate are only 48.97% and 10.00%, respectively. To address these limitations, we develop ObligationGuard using 40,000 synthetic training examples. ObligationGuard achieves 57.52% recall and an exact-match rate of 21.67%, surpassing all evaluated models on both metrics. | **[MS-Eval: Benchmarking Sub-Agent Delegation and Orchestration in MCP-Intensive Working Scenarios](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22869v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents are increasingly integrated into real-world works, yet they must handle diverse domain-specific tools and complex multi-sub-task problems that sequential tool invocation resolves inefficiently and with severe context bloat. Our case study further traces these gains to better single-agent solutions, effective context offloading, and genuine sub-task parallelism, whereas unnecessary or redundant delegation turns the same cost into pure overhead. Codes: https://github.com/LINs-lab/MS-Eval</p></details> | 48 pages, 20 figures |

An internal large language model (LLM) agent reads the task and evidence, selects a permitted tool action or request for human feedback, and uses checked results to decide whether to continue, revisit an analysis, switch a candidate, or stop. | **[Understanding KV Residency in Production-Scale Agentic LLM Workloads](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22863v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agentic large language model (LLM) applications turn one user request into a sequence of model and tool calls over a growing context. We characterize four production Microsoft M365 workload traces (Deep Research, Enterprise Assistant, Coding Agent and Enterprise Chat) spanning more than 121 million model calls. In Deep Research, the application issues 89% of model calls and the median context grows from about 10K to 68K tokens by the 30th call. About 80% of context tokens are served from the prefix cache. With the public Llama-2-70B KV layout standing in for confidential models, one 99th-percentile Deep Research session exceeds the 80 GB HBM of an NVIDIA H100, and holding all live sessions at their peak needs an estimated 501.8 TB of HBM, although inference occupies only 5.6% of that retention time. Trace-driven replay shows that a one-hour idle cap cuts this ceiling by 3.6x while raising the share of calls that recompute context only from 4.07% to 4.53%. Under tighter budgets, eviction alone is insufficient: at one tenth of the busiest hour's demand, LRU eviction makes 63% of Deep Research calls recompute and clairvoyant furthest-next-use 31%, whereas multi-resolution retention (MRR), which demotes the LRU session's KV state to int4, lowers this to 13.6%. Placement imposes a separate limit: when the next call reaches a different deployment, cached-context reuse drops from 89% to 29%, and treating KV state as deployment-local raises the recompute floor under unlimited memory from 5.4% to 20.2%. These findings motivate retention that changes the representation of KV state, together with deployment-aware routing or migration. | **[LoongSage: A Production-Grade, High-Efficiency Agentic RL Framework for Frontier LLMs with Validated Recipes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16352v2)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reinforcement learning (RL) has emerged as a critical phase in the post-training pipeline of large language models (LLMs). To address these challenges, we present LoongSage, an agent-native, fully asynchronous RL framework with a lightweight core of roughly 14K lines of code. Built upon Megatron for distributed training and SGLang for high-throughput inference, LoongSage centralizes orchestration within a single controller process and encapsulates all GPU-resident components as Ray actors. For asynchronous execution, it integrates sliding-window strategies that bound trajectory staleness, and employs TransferMesh, a topology-aware synchronization mechanism that routes weights after each step over the fastest transport available to each receiver. Beyond agentic RL, LoongSage incorporates a memory-efficient Teacher Manager that decouples the scaling of teacher models from GPU memory constraints, natively supporting Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation. Extensive evaluations across four complete training runs, spanning complex reinforcement learning and multi-teacher distillation, demonstrate that task rewards improve steadily while the train-inference gap is kept under continuous control, and the fully asynchronous pipeline removes synchronous idle bubbles, sustaining hardware utilization.</p></details> |  |

## Drug Design
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[DrugHarness: An Evolvable Agent Harness for Computational Drug Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23791v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Computational drug discovery requires agents to compose scientific operations and adapt to changing evidence and constraints. Here we introduce DrugHarness, an evolvable agent harness coupling composable scientific execution with conditional skill induction. Its environment integrates over 80 scientific operations, biomedical resources and task-specific code, preserving associations among candidates, results and artifacts. From execution trajectories and outcome feedback, DrugHarness induces action hypotheses with observable applicability conditions, recommended actions and supporting evidence. An independent review agent evaluates candidate skills derived from these hypotheses through source verification and, when needed, targeted execution trials. Admitted skills guide subsequent tasks and are revised through feedback. Evaluation on MolBench and SMDD-Bench demonstrates capabilities in molecular screening, optimization and constrained design. Without evolution, DrugHarness exceeds reported MolClaw-CC results by 10.8 percentage points in affinity-comparison accuracy and 55% in mean top-three active hits on MolBench. Retrospective evaluation on skill-development tasks shows that evolution increases Qwen's affinity-comparison accuracy from 62.2% to 81.1% and optimization success from 79.5% to 87.2%. In a prospective HPK1 study, DrugHarness achieves a more favorable median docking score than Biomni among system-specific candidates. A synthesized compound achieves a cellular IC50 of 14.19 nM, a 2.64-fold potency improvement over the best input reference, with activity competitive with two selected compounds designed by experienced medicinal chemists. These results support conditional skill evolution for scientific agents and demonstrate a workflow connecting computational drug design with experimental validation.</p></details> |  |
| **[Generative molecule evolution using 3D pharmacophore for efficient Structure-Based Drug Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.20130v2)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent breakthroughs in generative models have shown great promise for drug discovery, yet Structure-Based Drug Design (SBDD) faces two critical challenges: the scarcity of high-quality protein-ligand complex data and the practical synthesizability of generated molecules. While ligand-based approaches benefit from billion-scale small molecule datasets, they often lack the structural context of the target protein. To address these dual challenges of data scarcity and synthetic inaccessibility, we propose MEVO, a generative molecular evolutionary framework that operates within a synthesizable chemical space. MEVO introduces a reaction-aware generative model trained on reaction routes constructed by applying predefined reaction templates to Enamine Building Blocks. The model jointly generates molecules and corresponding synthesis sketches consistent with these templates. By integrating this generative capability into a pocket-aware evolutionary strategy, MEVO iteratively refines molecular structures within the synthesizable chemical space, guided by a hierarchical scoring function that combines fast approximation with rigorous Absolute Binding Free Energy Perturbation (ABFEP) calculations. This framework efficiently generates high-affinity, synthetically feasible binders for diverse protein targets. We validate MEVO's performance through rigorous ABFEP calculations, demonstrating its ability to design potent inhibitors for challenging targets such as KRAS$^{\textrm{G12D}}$, achieving affinities comparable to known active inhibitors. By integrating the data richness of ligand-based learning with the precision of structure-based optimization, MEVO offers a holistic solution that ensures both high binding affinity and synthetic accessibility, paving the way for more efficient and practical structure-based drug design.</p></details> |  |
| **[PMO-Dock: Benchmarking Docking, Specificity, and Generalization in Molecular Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19011v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The Practical Molecular Optimization (PMO) benchmark standardized evaluation in molecular optimization, but it is built on simple property-based oracles that do not capture structure-based drug design. As the field has moved to docking-based objectives, evaluation practice has become unstandardized, and different methods report results on different docking tasks, often without a shared protocol for fair comparison or sample-efficiency constraints. To address this, we introduce Practical Molecular Optimization for Docking (PMO-Dock), a benchmark and protocol for docking-based optimization consisting of 25 tasks covering hit generation, lead optimization, and a new specificity task requiring strong on-target binding while penalizing off-target interactions. The protocol separates model development from final evaluation: validation tasks are used for hyperparameter tuning, and test tasks are held out for final evaluation and are not used during development. Evaluation on the test tasks is done under a strict oracle budget to reflect realistic drug-discovery settings. We benchmark four diverse high-performing methods spanning different optimization paradigms, Saturn (reinforcement learning), GenMol (discrete diffusion), Genetic-guided GFlowNet, and ChemLlama (LLM-based evolutionary search). Our results show no universally best method across tasks, additional analyses of hyperparameter transfer (cross-task and cross-category) and sensitivity show that transfer is often limited and varies by method, motivating method-dependent selection strategies. This benchmark provides a concrete standard for evaluating generalization to unseen tasks and sample efficiency under a limited test-time oracle budget.</p></details> |  |
| **[A Large Scale Investigation of Scaling Limits in Chemical Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.13408v3)** | 2026-10-01 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Chemical Language Models (CLMs) are increasingly used in de novo drug design, driven by recent growth in model scale, compute, and dataset size. These representational gains coexist with diminishing improvements in goal-directed generation under the evaluated protocols and oracle budgets. | **[A Systematic Evaluation of Molecule Generation Models for De Novo Drug Design: From Benchmarks to Practical Insights](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10099v1)** | 2026-09-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Molecule generation has emerged as a powerful computational tool for de novo drug design, enabling the exploration of chemical space beyond the limits of conventional virtual screening. In this review, we present a comprehensive evaluation of molecule generation models for de novo drug design, covering 82 methods across five deep generative frameworks, including recurrent neural network (RNN)- and Transformer-based models, variational autoencoders (VAEs), generative adversarial networks (GANs), flow-based models, and diffusion models. Looking ahead, we discuss future directions in standardized 3D data, interaction-aware generation, receptor flexibility, and multi-objective molecular design, with the aim of improving the reliability and experimental relevance of molecule generation. | **[FuseDiff: Symmetry-Preserving Joint Diffusion for Dual-Target Structure-Based Drug Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.05567v2)** | 2026-09-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Dual-target structure-based drug design aims to generate a single ligand together with two pocket-specific binding poses, each compatible with a corresponding target pocket, enabling polypharmacological therapies with improved efficacy and reduced resistance. We compose the reward from a conditional value-at-risk (CVaR), a worst-case score rule, and an ambiguity radius expressing distrust in the stated weights. | **[Language-Informed Flow Matching for Trend-Guided Structure-Based 3D Molecular Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.31009v1)** | 2026-08-31 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Structure-based drug design (SBDD) requires ligands that satisfy both 3D target affinity and 1D chemical validity. | **[MCCE: A Framework for Multi-LLM Collaborative Search in Discrete Spaces with Similarity-Filtered Preference Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.06270v2)** | 2026-08-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-objective discrete optimization problems, such as molecular design, pose significant challenges due to their vast and unstructured combinatorial spaces. | **[READ: A Retrieval-Alignment Diffusion Framework for Structure-based Drug Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14488v2)** | 2026-08-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Structure-based drug design (SBDD) models are central to modern pharmaceutical research, enabling the rational exploration of protein-ligand interactions at atomic resolution. However, most existing approaches frame molecular generation as an isolated optimization or a one-to-one matching task, overlooking the shared binding patterns and intrinsic similarities among protein-ligand complexes. | **[FROGENT: An End-to-End Full-process Drug Design Multi-Agent System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.10760v2)** | 2026-03-01 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Drug discovery is a complex, multi-step pipeline that remains heavily dependent on manual, experience-driven operations; meanwhile, existing customized artificial intelligence tools are fragmented across web applications, desktop software, and code libraries, resulting in incompatible interfaces and inefficient, burdensome workflows. FROGENT is a collaborative multi-agent system comprising a central Orchestrate Agent for strategic workflow coordination and three distributed agents, Retrieve, Forge, and Gauge, that employ dynamic biochemical databases, extensible tool libraries, and task-specific computational models via the Model Context Protocol. This architecture enables end-to-end execution of complex drug discovery pipelines, covering target identification, small-molecule generation, peptide optimization, and retrosynthetic planning. Across eight benchmarks spanning core drug discovery tasks, FROGENT consistently outperforms six increasingly advanced ReAct-style agents. Case studies further demonstrate its practicality and generalization across real-world small-molecule and peptide design scenarios. Overall, FROGENT not only achieves substantial gains in efficiency and accuracy, but also demonstrates the potential of LLM-based agentic systems to autonomously orchestrate drug development pipelines, reducing, or even replacing, reliance on manual, experience-driven human intervention.</p></details> | 37 pages, 20 figures |
| **[MBD-ML: Many-body dispersion from machine learning for molecules and materials](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.22086v1)** | 2026-02-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Van der Waals (vdW) interactions are essential for describing molecules and materials, from drug design and catalysis to battery applications. Through seamless integration with libMBD, our method enables the immediate calculation of MBD-inclusive total energies, forces, and stress tensors. By eliminating the need for intermediate electronic structure calculations, MBD-ML offers a practical and streamlined tool that simplifies the incorporation of state-of-the-art vdW interactions into any electronic structure code, as well as empirical and machine-learned force fields.</p></details> | <details><summary>22 pa...</summary><p>22 pages, 6 figures, Supplementary Information (12 figures)</p></details> |
| **[Benchmarking Pretrained Molecular Embedding Models For Molecular Representation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.06199v4)** | 2026-02-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Pretrained neural networks have attracted significant interest in chemistry and small molecule drug design. Only the CLAMP model, which is also based on molecular fingerprints, performs statistically significantly better than the alternatives. DecompDPO introduces decomposition into the optimization objectives and obtains preference pairs at the molecule or decomposed substructure level based on each objective's decomposability. Additionally, DecompDPO introduces a physics-informed energy term to ensure reasonable molecular conformations in the optimization results. Notably, DecompDPO can be effectively used for two main purposes: (1) fine-tuning pretrained diffusion models for molecule generation across various protein families, and (2) molecular optimization given a specific protein subpocket after generation. Extensive experiments on the CrossDocked2020 benchmark show that DecompDPO significantly improves model performance, achieving up to 95.2% Med. High Affinity and a 36.2% success rate for molecule generation, and 100% Med. High Affinity and a 52.1% success rate for molecular optimization. Code is available at https://github.com/laviaf/DecompDPO.</p></details> | Accepted by TMLR |
| **[General Binding Affinity Guidance for Diffusion Models in Structure-Based Drug Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.16821v3)** | 2026-02-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Structure-based drug design (SBDD) aims to generate ligands that bind strongly and specifically to target protein pockets. Furthermore, we extend the framework to \textbf{multi-constraint diffusion guidance}, jointly optimizing for binding affinity, drug-likeness (QED), and synthetic accessibility (SA) to design realistic and synthesizable drug candidates.</p></details> |  |

| **[Agentic reinforcement learning empowers next-generation chemical language models for molecular design and synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.17687v2)** | 2026-01-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Language models are revolutionizing the biochemistry domain, assisting scientists in drug design and chemical synthesis with high efficiency. | **[SiDGen: Structure-informed Diffusion for Generative modeling of Ligands for Proteins](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.09529v3)** | 2026-01-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Structure-based drug design (SBDD) faces a fundamental scaling fidelity dilemma: rich pocket-aware conditioning captures interaction geometry but can be costly, often scales quadratically ($O(L^2)$) or worse with protein length ($L$), while efficient sequence-only conditioning can miss key interaction structure. | **[Dynamics-inspired Structure Hallucination for Protein-protein Interaction Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.06214v1)** | 2026-01-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Protein-protein interaction (PPI) represents a central challenge within the biology field, and accurately predicting the consequences of mutations in this context is crucial for drug design and protein engineering. We present in this work the first comprehensive experimental analysis of this advantage of QGANS applied to SAT4 image generation, obtaining an exponential advantage in capacity scaling for a quantum generator in the hybrid latent style-based QGAN architecture. Careful tuning of the autoencoder is crucial to obtain stable, reliable results. Once this tuning is performed and defining training optimality as when the training is stable and the FID score is low and stable as well, the optimal capacity (or number of trainable parameters) of the classical discriminator scales exponentially with respect to the capacity of the quantum generator, and the same is true for the capacity of the classical generator. | **[ReACT-Drug: Reaction-Template Guided Reinforcement Learning for de novo Drug Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.20958v1)** | 2025-12-24 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>De novo drug design is a crucial component of modern drug development, yet navigating the vast chemical space to find synthetically accessible, high-affinity candidates remains a significant challenge. ## multi-objective optimization
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[On the possibilities and limitations of LLM-based multi-agent systems for multi-criteria decision-making in healthcare operations management](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23673v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-criteria models for healthcare operations need weights that express how stakeholders trade off patient waiting time, provider workload, and operating cost, but these weights are difficult to quantify. Teams of large language model (LLM) agents, each prompted to represent a stakeholder, have been used to supply them. We propose a framework for assessing the reliability of LLM-generated preferences as inputs to multi-objective optimization and quantifying how their uncertainty affects decisions. We study explicit preferences by asking agents to rate the importance of the criteria in an outpatient appointment scheduling problem, and implicit preferences by inferring trade-offs from the agents' pairwise choices between emergency department configurations. The computational experiments show that the explicit preferences respond to the clinic's operating conditions and differ across roles, but deliberation among the agents can erase these differences, whereas simple averaging preserves them. The pairwise choices used to reveal the agents' implicit preferences depend largely on which option is presented first, and we find limited evidence that a statistical model using contextual information alongside presentation order predicts the agents' choices on new comparison pairs better than a model using presentation order alone. We then formulate robust weighted-sum optimization models to account for preference uncertainty. In most cases, these models select different schedules or configurations from those obtained using average preference weights. These results indicate that LLM-generated preferences need an audit of this kind before they are used as optimization weights in healthcare decision support.</p></details> |  |
| **[ParetoChat: An LLM-Based Interface for Interactive Multi-Objective Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23062v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-objective optimization models are often deployed in settings where decision makers (DMs) must balance some conflicting criteria. In practice, however, stakeholders must translate their qualitative preferences between different criteria into numerical weights, priorities, or reference points required by optimization methods, which can be difficult to specify and interpret. This paper proposes \emph{ParetoChat}, an LLM (large language model)-based expert-agent interface for interactive multi-objective optimization. The agent translates natural-language user input into structured scalarization parameters, invokes an optimization system, presents candidate solutions, and iteratively elicits feedback. We describe a multi-agent architecture that separates input processing, multi-criteria decision-making (MCDM) expertise, solution presentation, and feedback processing. | **[SIMS: Scale-Invariant Merit-Function-Based Scalarization for Multi-Task Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12599v1)** | 2026-09-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-task learning (MTL) requires navigating unavoidable trade-offs among competing objectives. We empirically find that existing merit-function-based scalarization approaches are sensitive to the relative scales of different objectives in practical MTL, where task losses commonly differ by orders of magnitude. The optimization process often favors objectives with larger scales even though the underlying Pareto optimal solutions remains invariant to rescaling (i.e., multiplying an objective by a positive constant). To address this issue, we propose Scale-Invariant Merit-function-based Scalarization (SIMS) for MTL. Specifically, SIMS adopts a transformation-induced merit function to convert the MOO problem of MTL to a single objective that renders optimization invariant to the magnitudes of losses. Theoretically, we prove that the requirement for scale invariance uniquely determines this transformation to be logarithmic. We further show that this general transformation-induced merit function preserves weak Pareto optimality and admits a smooth surrogate with controllable approximation error. Extensive experiments on representative multi-task benchmarks demonstrate that SIMS consistently outperforms existing scalarization methods and achieves state-of-the-art performance.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by the 32nd ACM SIGKDD Conference on Knowledge Discovery and Data Mining (KDD 2026)</p></details> |
| **[ParetoTransport: Generative Optimization by Mass Transport Toward The Pareto Front](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07706v1)** | 2026-09-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Offline multi-objective optimization requires not only moving the objective vectors of candidate designs toward the Pareto front, but also distributing them effectively along it. Existing methods, however, largely retain classical sample-wise guidance strategies, leaving the distribution-level modeling capability of generative methods underused. We propose ParetoTransport, a training-free guidance method for pre-trained flow-matching models that explicitly specifies and refines a population-level distribution in objective space. ParetoTransport guides a flow-matching sampler to iteratively transport the empirical offline distribution toward the Pareto front, with Wasserstein matching to intermediate proxy distributions. This directly controls distributional displacement and mass allocation along the front. We establish a convergence result and demonstrate state-of-the-art performance on standard offline MOO benchmarks, extending recent evaluations beyond hypervolume to generational distance, inverted generational distance, and Wasserstein distance.</p></details> |  |
| **[Learning to Construct Practical Agentic Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.00189v2)** | 2026-09-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Automated design and optimization of agentic LLM-based systems leads to sophisticated systems that substantially improve result quality over off-the-shelf agentic patterns. In addition, the simultaneous and explicit optimization of both interpretability and fairness, along with their trade-offs and the underlying reasons, remains underexplored. To address this, we propose a multi-objective neural basis model (MONBM) framework based on multi-objective evolutionary learning to consider accuracy, interpretability, and fairness simultaneously. | **[LLM4AIGQ: LLM-based AI Guidance Query Generation Framework for Multi Interest Mining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03674v1)** | 2026-09-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Guidance queries stimulate user consumption by extracting preferences to provide search queries with guidance value, playing a crucial role in the e-commerce field. In terms of model training, we employ a post-training pipeline comprising Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT), Reinforcement Learning (RL), and Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) to enhance the model's capability in generating AIGQ. | **[Data-Driven Design Optimization of Streaming-Potential-Mediated Electrokinetic Transport of Viscoelastic Fluids in Microchannels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29939v1)** | 2026-08-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Streaming-potential-mediated transport of viscoelastic fluids has attracted research attention owing to its applications in electrokinetic energy conversion and microfluidic transport. | **[ATLAS: Automated Approximation of Transformers for Efficient Homomorphic Inference in One Hour](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23478v2)** | 2026-08-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Fully homomorphic encryption (FHE) lets a server run inference on encrypted data with strong privacy guarantees, but running a Transformer under FHE is expensive. Its non-linear operations, such as softmax, normalization, and activation, must be replaced with polynomial approximations that the CKKS scheme supports, and the depth of these approximations dominates inference cost. Existing FHE Transformers use hand-tuned approximation settings, such as iteration count and polynomial degree, applied uniformly across layers, models, and tasks. Hand-tuning is slow and error-prone. Even a single uniform setting has about $10^7$ choices, and manual search cannot exploit layer-wise variation. AutoFHE, the only automated method with multi-objective search, targets ReLU-only CNNs and needs full fine-tuning per candidate, which is too costly for Transformers. Per-layer settings also push the search space to about $10^{85}$ for BERT and ViT and $10^{228}$ for LLaMA3, beyond both manual and fine-tuning-based search. We present ATLAS, a training-free framework that automates this search by treating each layer's approximation setting as a multi-objective optimization over latency and accuracy. The problem is hard: the decision space is large (96 or 256 variables), each configuration takes 70 to 1,000 seconds to evaluate even in cleartext, and 85 to 90 percent of configurations are invalid. ATLAS handles this with a two-stage optimization strategy and a surrogate model, completing the search in about one hour. Compared to an iterative softmax baseline, ATLAS cuts multiplicative depth and end-to-end latency by about 35 percent with little accuracy loss, and works across encoder-only, decoder-only, and vision Transformers, complementing parallel work on packing and matrix multiplication.</p></details> | <details><summary>Code:...</summary><p>Code: https://github.com/jianhayes/ATLAS</p></details> |

| **[Towards Efficient Pareto Set Approximation via Mixture of Experts Based Model Fusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.09770v2)** | 2026-08-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Solving multi-objective optimization problems for large deep neural networks is a challenging task due to the complexity of the loss landscape and the expensive computational cost of training and evaluating models. | **[BOCoDe: Engineering-Centered Benchmarking for Bayesian Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15073v1)** | 2026-08-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Bayesian optimization (BO) is a sample-efficient, surrogate-based approach to black-box optimization (BBO), but its evaluation remains dominated by synthetic functions and hyperparameter optimization (HPO) tasks that are typically low-dimensional and single-objective. ## DNA
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[Surveying Experimentally-Validated Components for DNA-Based Molecular Communication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23361v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Molecular communication is a novel paradigm for the design of nanonetworks. It uses molecules as information carriers and enables communication between nanodevices. DNA-based molecular communication is a promising approach for medical applications, such as in-body networks for diagnostics and drug delivery. However, the transition from theoretical models to practical experiments is still in its early stages. This survey reviews experimentally validated DNA-tiles, nanocarriers, and biosensors relevant to implementing DNA-tile-based nanonetworks in vitro. We evaluate these components based on their stability, compatibility, cost-effectiveness, and overall suitability for wet-lab implementation. Based on this analysis, we identify promising component combinations and derive a blueprint for a minimal proof-of-concept experiment. The resulting framework provides researchers with both a structured overview of experimentally relevant design choices and a concrete starting point for implementing and validating DNA-based nanonetworks in the wet lab.</p></details> | <details><summary>28 pa...</summary><p>28 pages, 7 Tables, 8 Figures, under submission for IEEE COMST</p></details> |
| **[JEPA-DNA: Grounding Genomic Foundation Models through Joint-Embedding Predictive Architectures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.17162v4)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Genomic Foundation Models (GFMs) typically rely on Masked Language Modeling (MLM) or Next-Token Prediction (NTP) to learn the "Laws of Nature". We introduce JEPA-DNA, a model-agnostic continual training framework that integrates a Joint-Embedding Predictive Architecture (JEPA) with traditional generative objectives. By supervising global sequence embeddings in a latent space, JEPA-DNA forces models to predict the functional representations of masked genomic segments, shifting the learning signal from token recovery to semantic alignment. We evaluate JEPA-DNA on 17 diverse genomic benchmark tasks, demonstrating consistent gains in linear probing and zero-shot performance regardless of the underlying GFM architecture or generative objective, and establishing a new state-of-the-art for GFMs under the linear-probing protocol. Through extensive ablation studies, we further characterize the synergistic interplay between generative and latent objectives. Our code is provided in the supplementary material.</p></details> |  |

We separately decompose embeddings into a component linearly predictable from 6-mer frequencies and a ridge residual, finding that linear recoverability does not reliably indicate downstream utility. Exact 6-mer-preserving shuffles further reveal task-relevant sequence-order sensitivity, most pronounced for NTv3, although order sensitivity alone does not guarantee predictive improvements. Our results establish compositional features as essential controls and offer complementary diagnostics for evaluating the added value of frozen genomic representations, without making claims about fine-tuning capacity.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to NeurIPS 2026 (Evaluations & Datasets Track). 9 pages of main text, 5 main figures, with references and appendices</p></details> |

| **[A Large Language Model LLM Approach to COVID-19 Fake News Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23800v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Text classification is a widely studied problem in information retrieval and data mining, with applications ranging from healthcare diagnosis to the detection of COVID-19-related fake news. Social media platforms generate vast volumes of user-generated text, and the rapid spread of misinformation during the pandemic has made automated fake news detection an important task. In this study, we apply pretrained transformer-based transfer learning (TL) models and large language models (LLMs), namely BERT-base, ALBERT-base, XLM-RoBERTa-base, DistilBERT, GPT-2, XLNet, and T5, to the COVID-19 English Tweets dataset, which contains 6,420 tweets labeled as real or fake. Models are evaluated using accuracy, precision, recall, and F1-score, and confusion-matrix heatmaps are used to visualize the predictions of each model. GPT-2 achieved the best performance, with an accuracy of 0.9631, precision of 0.9612, recall of 0.9610, and F1-score of 0.9600, while Nelder-Mead fusion achieved the best result among the fusion methods with an accuracy of 0.9610. We conclude by outlining future directions, including multiclass classification, multilingual datasets, and real-time social media streams.</p></details> | <details><summary>Pages...</summary><p>Pages 15, 10-15 Figures</p></details> |

Our results show that, on average, CSNB retains 99% of the fault detection capability of ABFT for the evaluated linear layers in dense LLMs, while reducing verification overhead by 87% and threshold determination overhead by 67%. For Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) router gate computations, however, CSNB retains only 55% of the fault detection capability of ABFT. These findings suggest that CSNB is a promising low-overhead alternative for protecting linear layers in dense LLMs, while checksum-based verification remains preferable for the evaluated MoE router gate.</p></details> |  |

For asynchronous execution, it integrates sliding-window strategies that bound trajectory staleness, and employs TransferMesh, a topology-aware synchronization mechanism that routes weights after each step over the fastest transport available to each receiver. Beyond agentic RL, LoongSage incorporates a memory-efficient Teacher Manager that decouples the scaling of teacher models from GPU memory constraints, natively supporting Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation. Extensive evaluations across four complete training runs, spanning complex reinforcement learning and multi-teacher distillation, demonstrate that task rewards improve steadily while the train-inference gap is kept under continuous control, and the fully asynchronous pipeline removes synchronous idle bubbles, sustaining hardware utilization.</p></details> |  |
