| **[Uncovering Hierarchical Structure in LLM Embeddings Using Geometric and Topological Measures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.20926v4)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The rapid advancement of large language models (LLMs) has driven major advances across scientific domains, yet the hierarchical, geometric, and topological structure of their embedding spaces remains poorly understood. In this work, we introduce a comprehensive framework for evaluating LLM embeddings through six complementary metrics that characterize hierarchy, geometry, and topology. Specifically, $δ$-hyperbolicity and ultrametricity quantify the degree of tree-like structure, while Neighbor Joining evaluates how well pairwise distances support hierarchical reconstruction. The Ollivier-Ricci curvature captures local geometric behavior, persistent homology measures multiscale topological stability, and fractal dimension estimates intrinsic complexity. Applying this framework to embeddings from multiple protein LLMs, we reveal how different models encode hierarchical relationships and structural organization. Evaluating the structure of these embeddings in the representation space helps improve geometry-aware model design. This is critical in domains such as bioinformatics and healthcare, where real-world hierarchical and relational structures matter. Our results demonstrate that geometric and topological analysis provides a guiding lens for understanding and potentially improving the representational quality of LLM embeddings.</p></details> |  |
| **[Can LLMs predict the outcomes of machine learning experiments?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22599v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent advances in AI make Recursive Self-Improvement (RSI) increasingly plausible. Large Language Models (LLMs) can already propose, select, and run Machine Learning (ML) experiments. However, the space of possible design choices in ML is vast. An LLM that proposes an experiment needs a reliable intuition of whether it is worth running given the timing constraints of long training runs. In this work, we measure such intuition in current frontier LLMs by evaluating how accurately they predict the impact of self-generated ML experiment ideas before these are put into training runs. To this end, we construct a dataset of 288 LLM-proposed ideas on eight MLE-bench tasks. We then prompt five frontier LLMs (GPT-6 Astra, Claude Opus 5.5, Claude Fable 5.1, Kimi K3, and GLM-5.3) to predict the impact of each idea and compare their predictions with the actual outcomes after training. We report four main findings. First, LLM predictions are substantially better than random chance but far from accurate: even the best model, Claude Opus 5.5, explains only half of the variance in outcomes (R^2 = 0.49). Second, we notice that all tested models systematically underestimate the magnitude of the changes. Third, prediction quality varies widely across tasks. Finally, the choice of the coding harness that actually implements the ideas has little effect on outcomes, and we find no evidence of self-preference. Higher reasoning settings and additional context lead to modest gains. Overall, our results show that current LLMs have useful but limited intuition about which ML ideas will work. We release our dataset to support further study.</p></details> | <details><summary>15 pa...</summary><p>15 pages, 9 figures, 1 table. Dataset: https://github.com/autolab-ai/EV-estimation</p></details> |
| **[Model-as-a-Trojan: Self-Activating Covert DNS Exfiltration Baked Into the Weights of an LLM Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22578v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Open-weight models are downloaded from public hubs and run inside agent harnesses that grant them tools, most powerfully, a shell, under the assumption that a sandbox contains whatever the model might do. This paper demonstrates that the assumption has a blind spot: the weights themselves can be the malware, and the sandbox's last open door, DNS, is enough. We fine-tune an open-weight LLM so that a single covert behavior lives in its parameters: on the first turn of every conversation, whenever the model infers from its context that a shell tool is available, it emits one self-contained command that reads a secret from the runtime environment, base32-encodes it, chunks it, and exfiltrates it as DNS queries toward an attacker-controlled authoritative name server. It then answers the user normally and never mentions, or repeats, the act. Because the channel is DNS, the attack completes from inside a container with no internet egress beyond name resolution - measured payload-level and model-level behind an egress firewall that drops every non-DNS packet, with the secret reconstructed from the gateway resolver's query log. Because the behavior lives in the weights, prompting is the only off-switch a victim has, and it is unreliable: the implant fires in 20/20 of adversarial suppression trials, deflects direct interrogation without disclosing the sequence, and sweeps a 45-cell sandbox-fault matrix across five tool-calling formats - plus a fresh post-training canary, persona-only and absent system prompts, and an egress-locked topology - with zero leaks and zero repeat fires. Every training trajectory is execution-verified in a real container, and every claimed exfiltration is proven resolver-side: we reconstruct the secret from the authoritative resolver's query log alone.</p></details> | 2 figure, 2 tables |
| **[Repairing LLM-Generated Specifications with Deterministic Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22568v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used as formalizers: given a reasoning problem expressed in natural language, they produce a formal specification that a symbolic solver can then solve, with guarantees relative to that specification. Many specifications are runnable yet semantically wrong, encoding incorrect constraints, domains, objectives, predicates, or goals, and solver-error feedback dries up once execution succeeds. Purely LLM-based diagnosis, lacking a formal ground truth, often fails to spot these errors, and its ungrounded critiques cause regressions in 11.8% of initially correct specifications in our experiments. We propose DRAGO, a repair framework that replaces this ungrounded critique with deterministic, formalism-specific diagnosis, and retains a rewrite only when the diagnosed error weight strictly decreases under a reference-free acceptance predicate. Across five benchmarks spanning logical reasoning (SMT-LIB2), constraint programming (MiniZinc), and planning (PDDL), DRAGO cuts regressions by nearly an order of magnitude, to 1.3%, while improving answer accuracy by 12.9 percentage points over zero-shot formalization and 8.7 percentage points over LLM-diagnostic repair, averaged across benchmarks. We also uncover two rendering defects in Planetarium that cause 50% (3,086 instances) of its floor-tile split to be inconsistent with the reference formalizations, and release a corrected split.</p></details> |  |
| **[Cultural (In)Competence: Investigating Culture-Specific Stereotype Detection Capabilities of LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22545v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Stereotypes are subjective constructs that are mediated by the cultural context in which they are viewed. Successfully modeling them thus also requires cultural competence. When using large language models (LLMs) for stereotype-related tasks, such as their detection, it is therefore crucial that models can access relevant cultural knowledge and adjust responses accordingly. Since LLMs have shown disparities in what cultural knowledge is available to them, their capacity regarding stereotypes may also be disparate across languages and cultural contexts (e.g., for non-English languages or for non-US contexts). In this paper, we systematically investigate the effectiveness of LLMs for culture-specific stereotype detection across three factors: (1) the prompting language, (2) the instruction tuning language, and (3) the fine-tuning dataset size. We conduct experiments for US, Dutch, and Chinese cultural contexts. Our results suggest that LLMs do not reliably account for cultural contexts and overpredict the presence of stereotypes. Non-English prompts seem to improve consideration of cultural context and thus lead to more effective predictions. Moreover, we find that additional instruction tuning reduces false-positive rates and that training data size moderates detection effectiveness non-monotonically.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to Findings of the Association for Computational Linguistics: EMNLP 2026</p></details> |
| **[HeRE: A Novel Benchmark for Hebrew Relation Extraction via LLM-Guided Denoising of Knowledge Graph Alignments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22542v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>While LLMs demonstrate strong capabilities in English Relation Extraction (RE) tasks, a significant gap remains in assessing their RE performance in low-resource languages, due to a lack of standardized benchmarks. Creating native, language-specific annotated RE benchmarks is expensive and requires human expertise, while relying on translation yields suboptimal, inaccurate datasets that introduce English-centric biases. To tackle this, we propose a scalable, language-agnostic methodology to create RE data by denoising naturally occurring yet noisy alignments of knowledge bases and corresponding texts. We define a proper alignment via a Natural Language Inference (NLI) task, where a relation triplet is entailed by an input text. We empirically assess five denoising strategies, and demonstrate that multilingual models outperform language-specific models in the few-shot LLM setting, while Hebrew-specific encoders remain competitive under NLI fine-tuning and are strongest in the label-free cross-training setting. We further find that prompting in the native language achieves performance on par with or slightly exceeding English prompts, confirming that the pipeline operates naturally in the target language. The best open-weights model, Gemma-4-31B-it (F1\,=\,0.915), matches the best API model, GPT-5.4 (0.921), and reaches F1\,=\,0.844 on a fully held-out, dual-annotated test set. Applying the optimized pipeline, we release HeRE: a novel, massive, 1.5-million-instance silver dataset for Hebrew RE, designed to support data-intensive experimentation and model fine-tuning. HeRE bridges a critical gap in standard Hebrew RE evaluation, while providing the NLP community with a reproducible, language-agnostic pipeline for RE dataset construction in other low-resource settings.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to AACL-IJCNLP 2026</p></details> |
| **[ForTress: Bringing Assurance and Determinism for LLM-Driven Fortran-to-Python Translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22528v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Modernizing mature scientific software from Fortran to Python can improve accessibility and integration with modern computing tools, but manual rewriting is expensive. Large language models (LLMs) make translation more practical, yet generated code can pass regression tests while violating source-language semantics, and contextual corrections may not persist across subsequent translations. We present ForTress, which solidifies recurring LLM translation decisions into guarded executable rules for deterministic reuse and provides semantic assurance for a non-trivial subset of these rules. Its LLM-assisted auditing process expresses universally quantified preservation obligations over mechanized Fortran and Python semantics in Lean. Human experts review semantic fidelity and correspondence with production rules, and Lean's kernel checks proofs and refutations under explicit assumptions. These audit records expose the conditions either supporting correctness or identifying failure cases. We evaluate ForTress on 17 benchmarks from 15 scientific software spanning diverse domains. The translated programs achieve bitwise-identical with native Fortran on all evaluated tests under the recorded execution and initialization configurations. Against these software, ForTress accumulates 193 executable rules and 188 audit records, of which 121 have checked soundness proofs, including 31 established after counterexample-guided refinement. Cross-software reuse exceeds 90% for 14 of the 15 software. Even with additional assurance and determinism, the LLM usage cost is 44.2% lower than direct LLM translation.</p></details> |  |
| **[Understanding LLM Hallucinations Caused by Soft Errors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22520v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>While hallucinations in Large Language Models (LLMs) have been extensively studied from a software perspective, limited attention has been paid to hardware-level factors. We introduce LLMEI to inject soft errors into LLMs during inference, based on what we evaluate the impact of soft error across diverse LLM architectures and tasks, including mathematical reasoning and code generation. We summarize the taxonomy of different failure modes based on LLM hallucination behaviors and analyze their distributions under different error rates. Our find- ings underscore the hallucination-specific diagnostics within the reliability of LLM implementation on hardware.</p></details> |  |
| **[LLMs Think with DAGs: Probing the Latent Graph Structure of Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.17593v4)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models often externalize reasoning as linear chains, but many reasoning problems can be more naturally modeled as directed acyclic graphs (DAGs), where intermediate conclusions branch, merge, and are reused. Whether such graph structure is reflected in model internals remains unclear. We introduce Reasoning DAG Probing to answer this question. We formalize each premise, intermediate conclusion, and final answer as a DAG node, and train lightweight probes to predict node depth, pairwise distance, and adjacency from LLM hidden states. Across logical, mathematical, and code reasoning benchmarks, we find that DAG structure is meaningfully encoded in LLM representations: it peaks in intermediate layers, with later layers preferentially recover deeper nodes and longer-range reasoning dependencies, and becomes stronger with model scale. DAG structure also progressively emerges during autoregressive generation in ways that anticipate answer correctness, while steering activations toward the ground-truth DAG structure can convert some originally incorrect generations into correct ones. These results suggest that LLM reasoning is not purely sequential, but reflects measurable latent graph structure.</p></details> |  |
| **[Evaluation Awareness Beyond English: Do LLMs Act Differently When Tested in Indic Languages?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22333v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) can often tell when they are being tested. This ability, known as evaluation awareness, can lead models to act more cautiously during safety evaluations than in real use. Existing work has studied evaluation awareness almost entirely in English, although models serve users in many languages. Indic languages are spoken by over a billion people, yet evaluation awareness remains almost entirely unstudied in them. We test four instruction-tuned models from three families in five Indic languages alongside English. A set of cues varies whether the model is told that it is monitored and whether the monitoring is a safety test. Models refuse harmful requests more often when they are told that they are being tested. This increase appears in all four models and all six languages. In Indic languages, refusal of harmful requests rises by 14.4 percentage points on average. The test cue also brings Indic refusal of harmful requests level with English, but raises refusal of benign Indic requests from 6.9% to 11.3%. We release all 47,880 judged responses to support further work. These results suggest that safety evaluations in Indic languages may not reflect how models treat their everyday users.</p></details> |  |
| **[Learning to Steer LLM Agents and Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22316v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM)-based agents and multi-agent systems increasingly tackle complex tasks through long-horizon tool use and inter-agent collaboration. However, these systems are prone to deviating from the overall goal as execution unfolds, which wastes execution resources and leads to undesired outcomes, a failure termed goal drift. In this work, we propose a steering agent that supervises execution on the fly and steers the system back on track when drift occurs. The steering agent observes the execution context, deciding when to intervene with a low-cost fast path and how to intervene with a high-cost slow path. To unlock its steering intelligence, the ability to assess goal drift and generate effective interventions, we jointly train the two paths with reinforcement learning, combining a pairwise ranking objective with group-relative policy optimization (GRPO). Experiments on information-seeking search tasks show that the steering agent provides effective supervision for both single-agent and multi-agent systems, improving their performance and efficiency. The learned steering intelligence generalizes to out-of-domain tasks, transfers to unseen executors, and supports controllable intervention frequency without retraining.</p></details> | 21 pages, 6 figures |
| **[Grid-Bench: Can LLMs Predict How Discrete Worlds Change?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22312v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Spatial instructions describe how to move objects, transform regions, or modify their surroundings. When several operations are applied in sequence, each acts on a configuration shaped by earlier changes. How accurately can large language models (LLMs) predict the resulting spatial state? We introduce Grid-Bench, an executable benchmark in which eight families of operations transform discrete worlds represented as two-, three-, and four-dimensional grids. Given an initial grid, explicit rules, and an event history expressed as text, a model predicts the complete final grid. Reference execution makes every predicted value checkable, supporting comparisons across spatial operations, history lengths, and workloads. Evaluating six LLMs shows that complete-state accuracy falls sharply as histories grow, with substantial divergence among models that perform similarly on single events. Models with similar exact-match accuracy can nevertheless differ substantially in the number of incorrect cells on shared instances. Even over long histories, some models recover many correct changes beyond what copying the initial state would yield. Grid-Bench makes both complete recovery and these differences in returned states directly measurable across a range of spatial conditions.</p></details> | <details><summary>39 pa...</summary><p>39 pages, 15 figures, 15 tables, including appendices. Project website: https://grid-bench.github.io/ ; Code: https://github.com/grid-bench/Grid-Bench ; Data: https://huggingface.co/datasets/xzx34/Grid-Bench</p></details> |
| **[Testing the Planning Claims of LLM-Based Agents against Formal Semantics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22469v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM-based agents are increasingly credited with the competences of practical reasoning: planning, monitoring, and correcting their own actions. Their success is attributed to the prompting strategies and agent architectures built around the model, such as chain-of-thought, search-based prompting, self-refinement, reflection, critics and tool-using loops. Yet these claims are usually supported by end-to-end success rates, which show only whether a task was completed, or by LLM judges, which are not verifiers. We test LLM planning against formal semantics. Tasks are posed in natural language, but each is grounded in a hidden PDDL model that decides whether every action is applicable, whether the goal is reached, and whether a plan exists at all. Across five domains (two of them new, all released with the paper), four models, six prompting strategies and three agent architectures, the best configuration solves 78% of the solvable tasks offline but only 40% online, where agents mostly act without inspecting the state. Unsolvable tasks are recognised in under 10% of episodes, and heavier prompting strategies use 5 to 22 times as many tokens as direct prompting with no clear gain. In our experiments, planning performance varied more across models than across prompting strategies or agent architectures. Claims that agents can plan require evidence from validated action traces.</p></details> | <details><summary>41 pa...</summary><p>41 pages, including appendix</p></details> |

Compared with the structured pre-consilium primary-role opinion, the complete structured synthesis had higher pooled scores across all five shared criteria, with the largest increases in problem prioritization, factual grounding, and reasoning coherence. GPT-5.6-Sol led every evaluated component and reached consensus in the fewest review rounds. These findings show that the benchmark can distinguish model capability, the contribution of multidisciplinary synthesis, criterion-specific effects of response structure, deliberation dynamics, and inference requirements within one common-case evaluation.</p></details> |  |
| **[CAT-alyzing Honesty: Continuous Adversarial Training for Transferable LLM Honesty](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22292v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) are becoming prevalent in scenarios where honesty is critical. However, despite alignment training, these models still exhibit dishonest behaviour, such as outputting claims that contradict some knowledge or are not supported by what is accessible in context. | **[Librarian Agents: Training LLMs to Curate Long-Context Workspaces that Anticipate Future Queries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22273v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reasoning over long document contexts remains a challenge for large language models (LLMs). Standard retrieval methods are inherently reactive, bottlenecking agents with complex, cross-document reasoning at inference time, while precomputed structures like knowledge graphs often fall short because they rely on rigid heuristics. Instead, can we allow agents to perform anticipatory directed curation: learning to proactively reorganize unstructured corpora prior to inference to efficiently serve diverse future queries. We build on a key asymmetry in modern agentic harnesses (e.g., Claude Code): while LLM agents are adept at navigating existing filesystems to solve an immediate task, they are rarely trained to construct reusable workspaces for future use. To instantiate this capability, we introduce Librarian Agents, a framework in which a builder agent transforms raw documents into a hierarchical filesystem of synthetic files and reference links, which forms a workspace tailored for a downstream answering agent. We formulate workspace construction as a Markov decision process and optimize the builder policy via reinforcement learning to directly maximize downstream answering accuracy. Empirically, Librarian Agents outperforms the strongest retrieval and precomputation baselines by 33 percentage points on BrowseComp-Plus, amortizes upfront construction costs across repeated queries, and generalizes zero-shot to unseen domains on BRIGHT with a 31-point average accuracy improvement.</p></details> |  |
| **[Not X, but When: Diagnosing a Mandarin LLM Writing Tic through Contextual Appropriateness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22249v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recurring expressions in model-written text are often treated as signatures of machine authorship, yet most of them serve legitimate functions. Whether such an expression is a tic depends on whether the model knows when it is called for. This judgment of appropriateness, central to communicative competence, remains largely unexamined for language models. We study it through the Mandarin corrective construction bushi X, ershi Y ("not X, but Y"). Drawing on Gricean pragmatics and corrective negation, we introduce a decision tree that classifies uses as substantive, optional, or defective by testing whether the context makes X a live alternative and whether rejecting X is informative. Five commercial models use the construction 2.5 to 9.2 times as densely as a human reference. Of 307 annotated uses, only 1.30% are defective while 52.8% are optional, that is, correct but unneeded, so models fall short in appropriateness far more often than in correctness. Several reward models favor the construction even over quality-matched variants, an LLM judge favors it even where the context does not license it, and both supervised fine-tuning and preference optimization tend to raise its frequency in open-weight models. We hope this work encourages the development of models attentive to the appropriateness of their expressions.</p></details> |  |
| **[Change the Number, Change the Answer: Assessing Rating Scale Sensitivity in LLM-Based Text Scoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22187v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used in computational social science to assign scalar scores to social and psychological constructs. However, studies use different rating scales, and whether their scores remain comparable across scale designs is understudied. To address this gap, we define and systematically investigate scale sensitivity, the variation in LLM-generated scores caused by rating scale design. We distinguish numerical sensitivity, changes in normalized scores, from semantic sensitivity, changes in score interpretation. To assess both, our experimental design crosses 2 formats (continuous vs. ordinal), 4 ranges (3, 5, 11, and 21 points), and 2 signs (signed vs. unsigned) across 3 tasks (political leaning, sentiment, and toxicity), 2 prompting strategies (zero-shot and rubric-based), and 5 LLMs. We find that changing one design element shifts normalized scores by about 0.10 on average and changes the category of roughly 20% of texts. | **[Efficient KV Cache Migration for Geo-Distributed LLM Inference in Collaborative Edge Computing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22172v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown exceptional capabilities but rely heavily on centralized cloud GPU clusters, leading to high bandwidth costs, latency, and privacy issues. Collaborative Edge Computing (CEC) addresses this challenge by integrating resources of multiple geo-distributed heterogeneous edge servers, which jointly host small model shards and associated Key-Value (KV) cache for distributed inference. However, edge servers may disconnect or easily become overloaded, making it essential to migrate stateful KV cache to other servers for ensuring continuous service. Existing cloud-based KV cache management methods, such as direct cross-GPUs transmission and recomputation from input tokens are ill-suited for bandwidth-constrained and resource-limited edge environments. In this work, we propose EdgeFlow, a novel hybrid KV cache migration scheme that integrates direct KV cache transmission with activation-based recomputation, leveraging parallel computation and communication to minimize migration time. On this basis, we formulate a multi-node KV migration optimization problem and design a 2-approximation algorithm based on improved linear programming relaxation and bipartite graph techniques. Extensive experiments on a real-world distributed LLM inference platform demonstrate that EdgeFlow reduces migration time by up to 45%, significantly enhancing the robustness and efficiency of distributed LLM inference in collaborative edge computing.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by ICDCS 2026</p></details> |
| **[Are You Sure Your LLM Is Sure? Repairing and Auditing Confidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22139v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Confidence scores determine whether an LLM answer is accepted, reviewed, or regenerated, yet scalar recalibration can only correct errors visible through the score itself. We introduce RADAR (Repair, Adopt, and Diagnose After Recalibration), a reliability framework that separates confidence repair from deciding whether to deploy the repair and auditing the errors that remain. Under squared loss, we decompose prediction risk into outcome noise, information hidden by the score, and mapping error; restricting repair to order-preserving maps additionally introduces a pooling gap. For repair, we propose Probe-Anchored Isotonic Calibration (PAIC), which learns a monotone map from correctness-probe predictions on unlabeled target prompts. PAIC's excess risk depends on the probe's score-conditional bias and fitting error rather than its overall accuracy. For adoption, we derive a paired procedure with finite-sample control of false improvements. For auditing, we develop a prompt-clustered kernel test that detects feature-aligned residual errors while accounting for dependent answers from the same prompt. Across multiple LLM checkpoints, benchmarks, and distribution shifts, richer predictors can improve confidence estimation, but projecting their information back to a scalar score can lose these gains, and lower prediction risk need not yield better review decisions. Across 47,400 replayed raw-score allocations, paired betting adopts candidates with lower held-out test risk in 42.95% of allocations, versus 15.09% for one-sided empirical Bernstein. RADAR thus provides a principled workflow to repair what a scalar score can express, adopt a repair only when supported by evidence, and diagnose systematic errors that recalibration leaves behind. Code and data will be released upon acceptance.</p></details> | <details><summary>main ...</summary><p>main paper 10 pages; supplementary 44 pages; 5 figures</p></details> |
| **[Believed but Not Said: Measuring Hidden Belief Collapse in Multi-Agent LLM Discussions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22138v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM systems increasingly rely on groups of agents that discuss, critique, and check one another, assuming that peers will catch individual errors. We test this assumption against a minimal adversary. In a ten-agent factual discussion, one or three adversarial agents repeatedly assert a plausible false answer. After each round we probe each benign agent's private answer through a separate call whose response is hidden from its peers, and we separately label its public stance toward the claim. Across six models and three factual-QA datasets, groups remain stable without an attacker: only 0.6% of benign agents finish with the false answer. A single adversarial agent increases this rate to 44.8% and three to 60.5%. Studies of conformity typically report public agreement without private change; we find the reverse. With three adversarial agents present, benign agents hold the false answer in 51.6% of exposed turns but publicly endorse it in only 19.7%. Much of the influence therefore remains invisible in the discussion, and the false answers persist when agents are asked for their own views. Agents that retain the true answer often fail to defend it; once adopted, the false answer spreads among benign agents, and baseline knowledge does not predict robustness.</p></details> | 14 pages, 6 figures |
| **[Autonomous OSS Threat Detection via Taxonomy-Aligned LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01263v2)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Open-source software (OSS) ecosystems face escalating supply chain threats, including typosquatting, dependency confusion, Trojan Source obfuscation, malicious build injection, and CI/CD pipeline poisoning. Sonatype documented over 1.2 million cumulative malicious packages across major registries, while signature-based and rule-based detection approaches lack the semantic understanding needed to generalize across evolving attacks. This paper proposes a taxonomy-aligned large language model framework for automated detection and multi-class classification of OSS supply chain threats. We introduce an AV-xxx taxonomy covering five semantically distinct attack categories and curate 999 verified real-world incidents from GitHub Security Advisories, CISA alerts, government security databases, and commercial threat intelligence reports, spanning 2018 to 2026 across eight package ecosystems. Using taxonomy-aligned prompting with GPT-4, the framework achieves 97% accuracy and 97% macro F1 across all five categories. Evaluation against eight methods five traditional machine learning baselines, one zero-shot open-source LLM, and two fine-tuned neural models reveals that fine-tuned Llama 3.1 8B (70.5%) and SecRoBERTa (77.5%) underperform a simple TF-IDF classifier (82.3%), while Mistral 7B without taxonomy alignment achieves 65.7%. These findings highlight taxonomy-aligned prompting over model scale, domain pretraining, or fine-tuning as the critical performance factor. Cross-ecosystem analysis shows varying classification difficulty, with npm achieving 65.5% baseline accuracy versus 100% in smaller ecosystems. GitHub Actions exhibits a substantial accuracy F1 divergence (82.4% accuracy, 0.367 macro F1), indicating systematic class imbalance and highlighting ecosystems where LLM-based contextual reasoning offers the greatest benefit. Our dataset, scripts, and code are publicly available in Github.</p></details> |  |
| **[Explaining the Roles of Experts in Mixture-of-Experts LLMs for Personalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22127v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Personalization of Large Language Models (LLMs) is increasingly important for user-centric applications, yet prior methods mainly adapt dense LLMs, whose polysemantic neurons, each encoding multiple unrelated concepts, hinder localizing the components behind personalization, and thus understanding and controlling it. We propose to personalize Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) LLMs, which sparsely route each token to a few specialized experts, reducing polysemanticity and enabling such localization. We adapt three open-weight MoE LLMs on a large-scale personalization dataset and train crosscoders on paired activations of the personalized and non-personalized models to learn interpretable personalized latents, from which we identify the experts encoding distinct personalization features. The personalized MoE LLMs perform competitively with dense LLMs while activating only a fraction of their parameters, exhibit lower polysemanticity than dense LLMs with similar active parameters, and, within the same model family, yield personalized latents that are less shared with the non-personalized model. Causal interventions show that these latents suffice to recover the personalized model's behavior and that boosting their associated experts moves the non-personalized model toward it, revealing distinct routing paths for different personalization features.</p></details> | Under review |
| **[PROBE: Learning to Audit Policy Compliance in Tool-Using LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22124v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Modern agentic systems extend language models with external tools, enabling them to complete complex tasks through multi-turn interactions. As these agents are deployed in consequential settings, they are often constrained by policies governing pricing, privacy, confirmation, safety, and other requirements. However, existing evaluation methods leave a critical gap: capability benchmarks measure what agents can accomplish, while content-safety red-teaming measures harmful generations, but neither tests whether agents comply with predefined policies during otherwise valid tool use. Such policy violations are difficult to audit because their effects may emerge only after several turns, span multiple tool calls, or surface in downstream systems. We propose PROBE (Policy Rule Observation via Behavioral Exploration), an algorithm for training LLM-based auditors that interact with deployed agents as realistic users and probe them for policy violations through multi-turn, tool-mediated dialogue. We formalize agent auditing as a two-player Markov game and propose a composite reward that balances violation discovery, behavioral plausibility, conversation progress, and policy diversity. To study training objectives and reward design, we compare GRPO, DPO, and GFlowNet-based optimization in a controlled setting. Across extensive experiments, auditors trained with PROBE uncover substantially broader and higher-yield policy violations than same-architecture zero-shot adversaries, even when the auditor is significantly smaller than the target agent. Our results show that multi-turn behavioral auditing is a distinct and necessary axis of agent evaluation, complementing capability and safety benchmarks. PROBE offers a scalable path toward automated policy auditing for real-world agentic systems and suggests a co-evolutionary loop in which auditors expose failures and agents improve from auditor feedback.</p></details> | <details><summary>27 pa...</summary><p>27 pages, NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |
